People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

I came in because my sleep has been really disrupted. I’m normally very disciplined about sleep, but since my cousin died, I lie awake replaying that night. It’s like that part of me has gone quiet.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your appetite since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed a clear change. I’m eating more or less like usual, I think. Maybe some meals feel a bit mechanical, but my appetite hasn’t stood out to me as different.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it difficult to function at work or in your daily life since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’m still going to the most essential meetings, but I’m not functioning at my usual level. I’ve had to hand off some community obligations and shorten my workdays because I don’t feel as steady or as effective with people as I normally am.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're struggling to connect with others or maintain your usual relationships since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’m still around people and doing what I’m supposed to do, but I don’t feel connected the way I normally would. At the prayer gatherings, and even when my nieces were trying to comfort me, I responded properly, but inside there wasn’t much warmth or relief. It feels like that affectionate part of me has gone quiet.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I may be a little less eager to be around people, but I haven’t really noticed myself steering clear of specific places or activities.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I feel awful that it happened, but I haven’t really been blaming myself for his death. It’s more sadness and shock than guilt.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a decrease in interest or pleasure in activities you normally enjoy since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I’ve still been showing up and doing what’s expected, but I don’t really feel much from things that should comfort me. At the prayer gatherings, or when my nieces tried to hug me or say something kind, I behaved normally, but inside I didn’t feel warmth or relief. It’s like that part of me has gone quiet.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your appetite since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed a clear change. I’m eating more or less like usual, I think. Maybe some meals feel a bit mechanical, but my appetite hasn’t stood out to me as different.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it difficult to function at work or in your daily life since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’m still going to the most essential meetings, but I’m not functioning at my usual level. I’ve had to hand off some community obligations and shorten my workdays because I don’t feel as steady or as effective with people as I normally am.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're struggling to connect with others or maintain your usual relationships since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’m still around people and doing what I’m supposed to do, but I don’t feel connected the way I normally would. At the prayer gatherings, and even when my nieces were trying to comfort me, I responded properly, but inside there wasn’t much warmth or relief. It feels like that affectionate part of me has gone quiet.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings and changes since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: About three weeks now. It started that night, when my cousin died in the highway collision, and it’s just been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this feeling of not feeling warmth or relief from things that should comfort you is something that happens occasionally or is it more of a constant state for you now?\n\nPatient: It’s more constant now. I can act the way I’m supposed to, like at the prayer gatherings or when my nieces try to comfort me, but inside I don’t really feel warmth or relief. It’s like that part of me has gone quiet.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or feeling restless and unable to sit still since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed that, no. I don’t think I’m moving or talking much slower than usual, and I wouldn’t say I’m pacing or unable to sit still. I’m just… going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're under a lot of emotional pain or distress most of the time since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say most of the time. I’m upset, yes, and it comes in waves, but I haven’t really thought of it as constant emotional pain. It’s just been hard.\n\nDoctor: Are there activities that you usually find enjoyable or fulfilling, but since your cousin's death, you've found yourself not wanting to do them?\n\nPatient: Yes. At the prayer gatherings, or even when my nieces tried to comfort me, I went through the motions and acted the way I should. But I didn’t really feel any warmth or relief from it. It was like the part of me that would normally feel affection or comfort just wasn’t there.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're moving or speaking slower than you used to?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I haven’t noticed myself moving or talking slower than usual.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It feels like that affectionate part of me has gone quiet.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings and changes since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: About three weeks now. It started that night, when my cousin died in the highway collision, and it’s just been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this feeling of not feeling warmth or relief from things that should comfort you is something that happens occasionally or is it more of a constant state for you now?\n\nPatient: It’s more constant now. I can act the way I’m supposed to, like at the prayer gatherings or when my nieces try to comfort me, but inside I don’t really feel warmth or relief. It’s like that part of me has gone quiet.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or feeling restless and unable to sit still since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed that, no. I don’t think I’m moving or talking much slower than usual, and I wouldn’t say I’m pacing or unable to sit still. I’m just… going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're under a lot of emotional pain or distress most of the time since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say most of the time. I’m upset, yes, and it comes in waves, but I haven’t really thought of it as constant emotional pain. It’s just been hard.\n\nDoctor: Are there activities that you usually find enjoyable or fulfilling, but since your cousin's death, you've found yourself not wanting to do them?\n\nPatient: Yes. At the prayer gatherings, or even when my nieces tried to comfort me, I went through the motions and acted the way I should. But I didn’t really feel any warmth or relief from it. It was like the part of me that would normally feel affection or comfort just wasn’t there.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're moving or speaking slower than you used to?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I haven’t noticed myself moving or talking slower than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're restless and can't sit still since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I’ve been unsettled at times, but I haven’t noticed that I can’t sit still or that kind of restlessness.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Then I wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning with these bits of hospital images in my head, and I’m going into board meetings feeling like I’ve only slept in pieces.\n\nDoctor: Do you have memories or thoughts about your cousin's death that keep popping into your mind throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s not constant, but it does happen. At odd moments, like if a phone rings late or I’m driving and headlights hit me a certain way, I suddenly see the hospital corridor in my mind, or I hear the police officer’s voice again. It sort of pushes in, and I have to stop for a moment and steady myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m avoiding things. I’ve still been going to work and handling what I need to handle. I may be a little less eager to be around people, but I haven’t really noticed myself steering clear of specific places or activities.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I feel awful that it happened, but I haven’t really been blaming myself for his death. It’s more sadness and shock than guilt.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a decrease in interest or pleasure in activities you normally enjoy since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I’ve still been showing up and doing what’s expected, but I don’t really feel much from things that should comfort me. At the prayer gatherings, or when my nieces tried to hug me or say something kind, I behaved normally, but inside I didn’t feel warmth or relief. It’s like that part of me has gone quiet.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your appetite since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed a clear change. I’m eating more or less like usual, I think. Maybe some meals feel a bit mechanical, but my appetite hasn’t stood out to me as different.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it difficult to function at work or in your daily life since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’m still going to the most essential meetings, but I’m not functioning at my usual level. I’ve had to hand off some community obligations and shorten my workdays because I don’t feel as steady or as effective with people as I normally am.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're struggling to connect with others or maintain your usual relationships since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’m still around people and doing what I’m supposed to do, but I don’t feel connected the way I normally would. At the prayer gatherings, and even when my nieces were trying to comfort me, I responded properly, but inside there wasn’t much warmth or relief. It feels like that affectionate part of me has gone quiet.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings and changes since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: About three weeks now. It started that night, when my cousin died in the highway collision, and it’s just been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this feeling of not feeling warmth or relief from things that should comfort you is something that happens occasionally or is it more of a constant state for you now?\n\nPatient: It’s more constant now. I can act the way I’m supposed to, like at the prayer gatherings or when my nieces try to comfort me, but inside I don’t really feel warmth or relief. It’s like that part of me has gone quiet.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or feeling restless and unable to sit still since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed that, no. I don’t think I’m moving or talking much slower than usual, and I wouldn’t say I’m pacing or unable to sit still. I’m just… going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're under a lot of emotional pain or distress most of the time since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say most of the time. I’m upset, yes, and it comes in waves, but I haven’t really thought of it as constant emotional pain. It’s just been hard.\n\nDoctor: Are there activities that you usually find enjoyable or fulfilling, but since your cousin's death, you've found yourself not wanting to do them?\n\nPatient: Yes. At the prayer gatherings, or even when my nieces tried to comfort me, I went through the motions and acted the way I should. But I didn’t really feel any warmth or relief from it. It was like the part of me that would normally feel affection or comfort just wasn’t there.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're moving or speaking slower than you used to?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I haven’t noticed myself moving or talking slower than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're restless and can't sit still since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I’ve been unsettled at times, but I haven’t noticed that I can’t sit still or that kind of restlessness.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe yourself as feeling empty or numb most of the time since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… numb is probably the right word. I’ve been doing what I’m supposed to do, like at the prayer gatherings, and when my nieces try to comfort me. But inside I don’t really feel warmth or relief. It’s like that affectionate part of me has just gone quiet.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're moving or speaking slower than you used to, or have you had trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I know you asked something similar before, and no—I haven’t noticed myself moving or talking slower, and I’m not really pacing around or unable to sit still either.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms lasted for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s been about three weeks now, since the night my cousin died in that highway collision.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
